What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Doc, it's... it's hard to explain, really, but my wife is the one who finally said I needed to come in, and I guess she's right. What brings me in is that I've just completely stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. And I mean *everything*. You see, I'm a carpenter, have been for years, and I used to love just walking into my shop in the morning. Not even to work, necessarily, but just to stand there with my coffee and enjoy the smell of the sawdust and the pine, you know? It used to center me. But lately, I don't even want to turn the key in the padlock. And it's not just work. We usually go to salsa nights on Thursdays—we've been going to the same club downtown for almost a decade, and we know all the regulars, but I haven't been in weeks. I just can't bring myself to put on my shoes and go out there. Same with church gatherings on Sundays. We're usually the ones who stay late stacking chairs, but now I can barely sit through the service without wishing I was back in bed. I even stopped cooking for the neighbors. We have this older couple next door, the Hendersons, and I usually smoke a brisket or make a big batch of chili for them once a month, but... I don't know, the thought of standing by the smoker all day just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't care about any of it anymore, and it's starting to scare me, to be honest. It's like the color has just drained out of everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like you've lost interest in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... it’s been, gosh, I’d say right around two months now. Yeah, about eight weeks. It all started right around the time my wife’s health took a sudden turn. It was so out of the blue, you know? One day everything is fine, we’re doing our normal thing, and the next, it's just... everything is turned upside down. \n\nWe’ve always been real sticklers for our routine, see. For years, we had our days planned out almost to the letter. I’d go to the shop early, she’d have her schedule, we’d always have lunch together at noon sharp, and then the evenings were our time to unwind. But when her health declined so suddenly... well, that whole routine just went right out the window. Suddenly we were dealing with all this new stress, and she needed more help around the house, and our day-to-day life just completely changed overnight. \n\nAnd I think that’s when this whole... this change in my mood and my behavior really started. At first, I just thought I was exhausted from worrying about her and from our schedule being so out of whack. I figured, you know, any husband would be tired and maybe not in the mood to go out dancing or socialize. But it’s been two solid months now. Even as we've sort of adjusted to the new normal with her health, I’m still just... stuck. I kept waiting to bounce back and want to go back to the shop or get back to the smoker for the neighbors, but it’s been two whole months of just feeling completely blank. It’s like the moment our routine broke, something inside me just shut off, and I haven't been able to flip the switch back on since.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Well, I really couldn't tell you for sure, because I'm just not the type to step on a scale very often. If we'd been out to the diner a few too many times, she'd tease me and say my tool belt was looking a little snug, and then we'd just cut back on the heavy dinners for a week or two. But with everything going on with her health lately, neither of us has been paying one bit of attention to waistlines. We've got bigger fish to fry, you know? \n\nI mean, my clothes still fit exactly the same, near as I can tell. And like I was saying before, our whole daily routine is just completely shot. We used to sit down for lunch at noon sharp, usually a sandwich or maybe some of that leftover chili I'd make for the neighbors, and now... well, we just kind of eat whatever is around whenever we finally realize we ought to. But even with all that disruption, and me not having any desire to stand at the stove and cook like I used to, I haven't noticed my pants getting any looser or tighter. So, no, I'd have to say my weight has probably stayed pretty much right where it always is. It's almost funny, really, how your whole life can feel completely turned upside down, but something like that just stays exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you were dead, over these past two months?\n\nPatient: Well, doc... that's a heavy question, isn't it? It's not something I ever thought I'd be sitting in a chair like this talking about. But... yeah. Yeah, I have. And I want to be clear, because it's not like I'm sitting in the shop staring at the table saw or anything like that. It's more of a... a quiet kind of dark thought that just sort of creeps in when I'm not looking. \n\nLike, the other morning, for instance. It was maybe Tuesday? Or Wednesday. The days kind of blur together now without the routine. But I woke up, and my wife was still asleep, and it was raining outside—that slow, heavy kind of rain that makes the whole house feel damp. And I just lay there staring at the ceiling fan, and this thought just washed over me, clear as day. I just thought... maybe it would be easier if I just didn't wake up at all. Just... drifted off and stayed there. No more worrying about her health, no more feeling this heavy, blank emptiness where my life used to be. Just... peace, I guess. \n\nAnd then, sometimes, it goes a step further, and that's the part that really shakes me up. I'll be sitting on the edge of the bed, trying to work up the energy to just put my socks on, and I'll catch myself thinking that maybe... maybe my wife and the kids—well, they're grown now, but you know what I mean—maybe they'd actually be better off without me dragging them down. I mean, look at me. I'm no help to her right now. I can't even make a pot of chili for the Hendersons next door, let alone be the rock she needs while she's sick. I just sit around like a lump. So my brain starts telling me, \"Hey, you're just a burden now. They'd be fine without you. Better than fine.\" \n\nAnd I know, logically, that's a terrible thing to think. It scares the living daylights out of me when those thoughts pop into my head, because I've always been the guy who fixes things, you know? If a porch step is rotted, I replace it. If the car makes a noise, I figure it out. But I can't fix this, and these thoughts... they just keep circling back, like buzzards. It's not that I want to, you know, do something drastic right this second, but just the fact that my mind keeps going to that dark place, thinking it would be easier to just... not be here... it's terrifying. It really is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get started on things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s... it’s really something that you bring that up, because it’s not just me noticing it anymore. The guys at the job, they’ve actually started saying things to me about it. I’ve been a carpenter a long time, right? Over thirty years. I know my way around a job site, and I've always been the guy who sets the pace for the younger crew. But lately... gosh, it’s literally like my work boots are just filled to the brim with wet cement. Every single step is just so incredibly heavy. \n\nI’ll be standing there, and I just need to grab a piece of lumber to run through the saw, and it’s taking me forever just to bend down and pick up a single board. I'll just stare at it. I know I need to pick it up, I know exactly what cut I need to make, but my body just doesn't want to cooperate. It’s like wading through molasses just to get from the back of my truck to the sawhorses. The foreman even asked me if my arthritis was acting up, but it's not my joints. It's just... everything. \n\nAnd then, if I'm not moving like a snail, it’s like... well, it’s this other thing that happens, which is almost worse. I'll get this weird, restless energy, but not a productive kind of energy. I’ll just get completely stuck pacing around the shop. I'll walk circles around the workbenches, back and forth from the tool chests to the door, just rubbing my hands together over and over. I'm not cold, I'm just... rubbing them, pacing, and not actually doing anything useful at all. \n\nOne of the apprentices actually stopped me on Thursday—or maybe it was Friday—and asked if I’d lost my favorite tape measure, because I must have walked past his station six times just wringing my hands and staring at the floor. I made up some excuse about trying to figure out a tricky angle for the crown molding, but the truth is, I just couldn't settle down to work, and I couldn't stop walking either. It’s incredibly frustrating, you know? To go from being the most efficient guy on the site to someone who either can't lift a piece of pine or is just wearing a trench in the floorboards accomplishing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty... yeah. That’s... gosh, that’s probably the biggest thing of all right now. It just eats at me, doc, from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to pass out at night. Because, you know, when you take those vows, your main job is to protect your family, right? To keep them safe from harm. And with my wife being sick... I just can’t stop my brain from spinning. \n\nI keep replaying every single doctor's appointment we went to, every little change in her health over the last year or however long it’s been. I sit there in the living room, and I just run through it all like a movie tape, over and over again. Like, I think back to when she first said she was feeling a bit rundown, and I just brushed it off. I told her, 'Oh, honey, it's just the change of the seasons, everyone's getting a bug.' Why didn't I push her to go to the clinic right then and there? Or that time she looked so pale just sitting at the kitchen table... I just made her a cup of tea. A cup of tea! \n\nI keep telling myself that a better husband—a guy who was actually paying attention, who actually took care of his wife the way he’s supposed to—he would have caught it so much sooner. He would have noticed something was really wrong and demanded they run tests, or... or just protected her somehow from all this pain she has to deal with now. I'm supposed to be the guy who fixes things. I can frame a whole house from the foundation up, but I couldn't even see that my own wife was getting sick right in front of my eyes. It’s just this constant, heavy guilt. I should have known, you know? I really should have done something to stop it, and I'll never forgive myself for missing all those signs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time over these past two months?\n\nPatient: Anxious... well, I’ve been thinking about that word, actually. Because my sister, she gets anxious. She’s the type who, if the wind blows too hard, she thinks the whole roof is coming off. She’s always been like that, ever since we were kids. But for me... no, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or on edge. Not really. \n\nWhen I hear \"on edge,\" I think about being nervous, you know? Like you're just waiting for the other shoe to drop. Like when you're framing a roof and the weather's turning fast, and the wind is picking up, and you're just praying you can get the tarp down and secured before the skies completely open up and ruin the plywood. That’s being on edge. Your heart is pounding, you're looking over your shoulder, you're keyed up. But this? What I’ve been dealing with these last couple of months? If the house phone rings, or if someone drops a whole stack of two-by-fours at the shop, I don’t even flinch. I just sort of... stare at it. I'm not sitting around biting my nails or worrying about what's going to happen tomorrow, because honestly, I barely have the energy to even care about tomorrow. \n\nAnd I know that pacing I told you about earlier, with the hand-wringing and wearing a hole in the floorboards... I know that probably sounds like I'm a nervous wreck, but my head isn't actually filled with anxious, panicky thoughts when I do it. It’s just this dumb, frustrating physical restlessness. You can't really be on the edge of the cliff if you've already fallen all the way down to the bottom. So, no, I really wouldn't say anxious is the right word for it at all. Gosh, I wish. No, not... not for a single minute, really. I keep waiting for that morning where I wake up and things feel a little lighter, you know? Like when a storm finally breaks and the barometric pressure drops. Because usually, if a guy gets into a funk, you at least have a good day mixed in there somewhere. A Friday where you knock off work early and the sun is shining and suddenly you feel like yourself again. But these last two months? It’s just been this one, long, continuous slog. \n\nThere hasn't been a single afternoon where I felt like the old me. I wake up at 5:15 every morning—I've always been an early riser, never really needed an alarm clock, just my body's natural rhythm after thirty years of construction—and before my feet even hit the bedroom floor, I'm already doing this mental check. I tell myself that maybe today is the day my legs won't feel like lead. Maybe today I won't just sit on the edge of the mattress staring at my boots for twenty minutes before I can even figure out how to put them on. But then I go to stand up, and... nope. It's right there waiting for me. Every single day. \n\nEven on the weekends, doc. You'd think, okay, Saturday rolls around, you don't have to go to the job site, you can just putter around the garage, maybe fix that squeaky hinge on the back door that's been bothering my wife... but I can't even manage that. I honestly can't point to a single hour, let alone a whole day, in the last eight or nine weeks where I felt like things were lifting or going away. It's just been a straight, flat line right at the absolute bottom, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of actually taking your own life, or made any plans to do so?\n\nPatient: Taking my own life... gosh. That's... well, I want to be completely honest with you here, doc. As far as making actual plans? Like, sitting down and figuring out a way to do it, or going out to the garage and looking for a way to end it all? No. I haven't done anything like that. I haven't made any plans. \n\nBut... the thoughts. That's a different story. I've definitely had thoughts. Like, if I could just go to sleep and the good Lord just decided it was my time, and I didn't have to open my eyes and face another day of this... this crushing weight. It just feels like it would be such a relief to not have to carry it anymore, to not have to face that guilt. \n\nAnd then my mind, it just keeps spinning, and I start looking at my wife, and how much she's dealing with, and I actually catch myself thinking that my family might be better off without me. I think to myself, maybe she wouldn't have to worry about me on top of her own health issues if I was just gone. Maybe the life insurance money would do more for her than I can right now. \n\nAnd I've got to tell you, doc, having thoughts like that... it scares me. It scares the living daylights out of me. I've always been a fighter, a guy who builds things up, you know? I frame houses to last a hundred years. I'm a protector. I don't tear things down. So to have my own brain telling me that my wife would be better off if I was dead... it's terrifying. It really rattles a man to his core to realize his mind is going to a place that dark. So, no plans, but... yeah, those thoughts are there, and they're really, really hard to shake once they get inside your head.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable changes in your physical activity level or movement over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Physical movement... yeah, well, I guess I kind of touched on that a little bit earlier when I mentioned the pacing, didn't I? It's like I've got two completely different speeds lately, and neither of them is any good for getting a house built. \n\nHalf the time, I'm moving like my boots are full of wet cement. Like I was saying before, doc, it's been completely constant. Every single day. No, doc, no. Not even close. Like I said, it's been constant. Every single day for two months. There hasn't been a two-week period where I felt better, there hasn't even been a two-*hour* period where I felt better. 

I know some guys, they get down in the dumps, and maybe they have a good weekend, you know? They watch the game, have a beer, and they snap out of it for a little bit. Or they go fishing and clear their head. But not me. This thing, whatever it is, it's just wrapped around me like a wet tarp, and it does not let up.

I keep hoping I'll get just a little break from the pacing, or a break from feeling like my boots are made of lead, or a break from those terrible thoughts about my wife being better off without me. But it just doesn't happen. 

Every morning, 5:15, my eyes open, and boom, it's right there waiting for me. The exact same heavy, crushing feeling. The exact same guilt. The exact same slowness. It's like being stuck in a rut that's so deep you can't even see over the edges, and the tires are just spinning and spinning, day after day, week after week. So no, no two-week breaks. No breaks at all. It's just been one long, unbroken stretch of this miserable, heavy feeling ever since her health went south.

And I just lay there staring at the ceiling fan, and this thought just washed over me, clear as day. I just thought... maybe it would be easier if I just didn't wake up at all. I made up some excuse about trying to figure out a tricky angle for the crown molding, but the truth is, I just couldn't settle down to work, and I couldn't stop walking either. It’s incredibly frustrating, you know? To go from being the most efficient guy on the site to someone who either can't lift a piece of pine or is just wearing a trench in the floorboards accomplishing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty... yeah. That’s... gosh, that’s probably the biggest thing of all right now. It just eats at me, doc, from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to pass out at night. Because, you know, when you take those vows, your main job is to protect your family, right? To keep them safe from harm. And with my wife being sick... I just can’t stop my brain from spinning. \n\nI keep replaying every single doctor's appointment we went to, every little change in her health over the last year or however long it’s been. I'm supposed to be the guy who fixes things. I can frame a whole house from the foundation up, but I couldn't even see that my own wife was getting sick right in front of my eyes. Like when you're framing a roof and the weather's turning fast, and the wind is picking up, and you're just praying you can get the tarp down and secured before the skies completely open up and ruin the plywood. That’s being on edge. Your heart is pounding, you're looking over your shoulder, you're keyed up. But this? What I’ve been dealing with these last couple of months? It’s not like that at all. \n\nIt’s actually kind of the exact opposite, if that makes any sense. I’m not jumpy in the slightest. If the house phone rings, or if someone drops a whole stack of two-by-fours at the shop, I don’t even flinch. I just sort of... stare at it. I'm not sitting around biting my nails or worrying about what's going to happen tomorrow, because honestly, I barely have the energy to even care about tomorrow. \n\nAnd I know that pacing I told you about earlier, with the hand-wringing and wearing a hole in the floorboards... I know that probably sounds like I'm a nervous wreck, but my head isn't actually filled with anxious, panicky thoughts when I do it. It’s just this dumb, frustrating physical restlessness. The heaviness, the guilt I was talking about, the pacing... none of it takes a day off. I honestly can't point to a single hour, let alone a whole day, in the last eight or nine weeks where I felt like things were lifting or going away. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, doc, I haven't. Not for two weeks, not for two days, not even for an afternoon. It's just... it's like I was just saying, it's just this one long, continuous stretch of misery. 

You know, you hear people talk about having a good day or a bad day, right? Like, "Oh, Tuesday was rough, but Wednesday was better." But for me, the days don't even have names anymore. It's just one continuous blur of this heavy, awful feeling. I'll be sitting on the edge of the bed, staring at my work boots, and I'll think, "Maybe today's the day. Maybe today I'll lace these up and feel like myself again. Maybe I'll go to the site and actually want to talk to the guys, or maybe I'll come home and be able to be there for my wife the way she needs me to be." 

But it never happens. Not once in these past two months. I haven't had a single moment where I felt like the old me—the guy who could build a house from the ground up and still have energy to cook dinner. It's just... it's always there. Like a shadow that won't go away, no matter how much light you shine on it. I haven't felt completely back to normal for a single second since this whole thing started.

What brings me in is that I've just completely stopped wanting to do the things that usually keep me going. And I mean *everything*. You see, I'm a carpenter, have been for years, and I used to love just walking into my shop in the morning. Not even to work, necessarily, but just to stand there with my coffee and enjoy the smell of the sawdust and the pine, you know? It used to center me. But lately, I don't even want to turn the key in the padlock. And it's not just work. We usually go to salsa nights on Thursdays—we've been going to the same club downtown for almost a decade, and we know all the regulars, but I haven't been in weeks. I just can't bring myself to put on my shoes and go out there. Same with church gatherings on Sundays. We're usually the ones who stay late stacking chairs, but now I can barely sit through the service without wishing I was back in bed. I even stopped cooking for the neighbors. We have this older couple next door, the Hendersons, and I usually smoke a brisket or make a big batch of chili for them once a month, but... I don't know, the thought of standing by the smoker all day just feels like climbing a mountain. I just don't care about any of it anymore, and it's starting to scare me, to be honest. It's like the color has just drained out of everything.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way, like you've lost interest in the things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, let me think about that... it’s been, gosh, I’d say right around two months now. Yeah, about eight weeks. It all started right around the time my wife’s health took a sudden turn. It was so out of the blue, you know? One day everything is fine, we’re doing our normal thing, and the next, it's just... everything is turned upside down. \n\nWe’ve always been real sticklers for our routine, see. For years, we had our days planned out almost to the letter. I’d go to the shop early, she’d have her schedule, we’d always have lunch together at noon sharp, and then the evenings were our time to unwind. But when her health declined so suddenly... well, that whole routine just went right out the window. Suddenly we were dealing with all this new stress, and she needed more help around the house, and our day-to-day life just completely changed overnight. \n\nAnd I think that’s when this whole... this change in my mood and my behavior really started. At first, I just thought I was exhausted from worrying about her and from our schedule being so out of whack. I figured, you know, any husband would be tired and maybe not in the mood to go out dancing or socialize. But it’s been two solid months now. Even as we've sort of adjusted to the new normal with her health, I’m still just... stuck. I kept waiting to bounce back and want to go back to the shop or get back to the smoker for the neighbors, but it’s been two whole months of just feeling completely blank. It’s like the moment our routine broke, something inside me just shut off, and I haven't been able to flip the switch back on since.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant change in your weight over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Weight? Well, I really couldn't tell you for sure, because I'm just not the type to step on a scale very often. We have this old dial scale in the master bathroom—the kind you have to tap with your toe to make sure the little red line zeroes out before you step on it—but honestly, I think it's just been gathering dust under the towel rack for years now. \n\nMy wife has always been the one who kind of kept an eye on that sort of thing for me. If we'd been out to the diner a few too many times, she'd tease me and say my tool belt was looking a little snug, and then we'd just cut back on the heavy dinners for a week or two. But with everything going on with her health lately, neither of us has been paying one bit of attention to waistlines. We've got bigger fish to fry, you know? \n\nI mean, my clothes still fit exactly the same, near as I can tell. My favorite pair of work jeans, the heavy canvas ones I always wear when I'm out in the shop, they still button up just fine without me having to suck my gut in or pull my belt any tighter. And like I was saying before, our whole daily routine is just completely shot. We used to sit down for lunch at noon sharp, usually a sandwich or maybe some of that leftover chili I'd make for the neighbors, and now... well, we just kind of eat whatever is around whenever we finally realize we ought to. But even with all that disruption, and me not having any desire to stand at the stove and cook like I used to, I haven't noticed my pants getting any looser or tighter. So, no, I'd have to say my weight has probably stayed pretty much right where it always is. It's almost funny, really, how your whole life can feel completely turned upside down, but something like that just stays exactly the same.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having any thoughts about death, or wishing you were dead, over these past two months?\n\nPatient: Well, doc... that's a heavy question, isn't it? It's not something I ever thought I'd be sitting in a chair like this talking about. But... yeah. Yeah, I have. And I want to be clear, because it's not like I'm sitting in the shop staring at the table saw or anything like that. It's more of a... a quiet kind of dark thought that just sort of creeps in when I'm not looking. \n\nLike, the other morning, for instance. It was maybe Tuesday? Or Wednesday. The days kind of blur together now without the routine. But I woke up, and my wife was still asleep, and it was raining outside—that slow, heavy kind of rain that makes the whole house feel damp. And I just lay there staring at the ceiling fan, and this thought just washed over me, clear as day. I just thought... maybe it would be easier if I just didn't wake up at all. Just... drifted off and stayed there. No more worrying about her health, no more feeling this heavy, blank emptiness where my life used to be. Just... peace, I guess. \n\nAnd then, sometimes, it goes a step further, and that's the part that really shakes me up. I'll be sitting on the edge of the bed, trying to work up the energy to just put my socks on, and I'll catch myself thinking that maybe... maybe my wife and the kids—well, they're grown now, but you know what I mean—maybe they'd actually be better off without me dragging them down. I mean, look at me. I'm no help to her right now. I can't even make a pot of chili for the Hendersons next door, let alone be the rock she needs while she's sick. I just sit around like a lump. So my brain starts telling me, \"Hey, you're just a burden now. They'd be fine without you. Better than fine.\" \n\nAnd I know, logically, that's a terrible thing to think. It scares the living daylights out of me when those thoughts pop into my head, because I've always been the guy who fixes things, you know? If a porch step is rotted, I replace it. If the car makes a noise, I figure it out. But I can't fix this, and these thoughts... they just keep circling back, like buzzards. It's not that I want to, you know, do something drastic right this second, but just the fact that my mind keeps going to that dark place, thinking it would be easier to just... not be here... it's terrifying. It really is.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed yourself moving more slowly or feeling like it's harder to get started on things than it used to be?\n\nPatient: You know, it’s... it’s really something that you bring that up, because it’s not just me noticing it anymore. The guys at the job, they’ve actually started saying things to me about it. I’ve been a carpenter a long time, right? Over thirty years. I know my way around a job site, and I've always been the guy who sets the pace for the younger crew. But lately... gosh, it’s literally like my work boots are just filled to the brim with wet cement. Every single step is just so incredibly heavy. \n\nI’ll be standing there, and I just need to grab a piece of lumber to run through the saw, and it’s taking me forever just to bend down and pick up a single board. I'll just stare at it. I know I need to pick it up, I know exactly what cut I need to make, but my body just doesn't want to cooperate. It’s like wading through molasses just to get from the back of my truck to the sawhorses. The foreman even asked me if my arthritis was acting up, but it's not my joints. It's just... everything. \n\nAnd then, if I'm not moving like a snail, it’s like... well, it’s this other thing that happens, which is almost worse. I'll get this weird, restless energy, but not a productive kind of energy. I’ll just get completely stuck pacing around the shop. I'll walk circles around the workbenches, back and forth from the tool chests to the door, just rubbing my hands together over and over. I'm not cold, I'm just... rubbing them, pacing, and not actually doing anything useful at all. \n\nOne of the apprentices actually stopped me on Thursday—or maybe it was Friday—and asked if I’d lost my favorite tape measure, because I must have walked past his station six times just wringing my hands and staring at the floor. I made up some excuse about trying to figure out a tricky angle for the crown molding, but the truth is, I just couldn't settle down to work, and I couldn't stop walking either. It’s incredibly frustrating, you know? To go from being the most efficient guy on the site to someone who either can't lift a piece of pine or is just wearing a trench in the floorboards accomplishing absolutely nothing.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel guilty about things that you think you've done or haven't done?\n\nPatient: Guilty... yeah. That’s... gosh, that’s probably the biggest thing of all right now. It just eats at me, doc, from the minute I wake up to the minute I finally manage to pass out at night. Because, you know, when you take those vows, your main job is to protect your family, right? To keep them safe from harm. And with my wife being sick... I just can’t stop my brain from spinning. \n\nI keep replaying every single doctor's appointment we went to, every little change in her health over the last year or however long it’s been. I sit there in the living room, and I just run through it all like a movie tape, over and over again. Like, I think back to when she first said she was feeling a bit rundown, and I just brushed it off. I told her, 'Oh, honey, it's just the change of the seasons, everyone's getting a bug.' Why didn't I push her to go to the clinic right then and there? Or that time she looked so pale just sitting at the kitchen table... I just made her a cup of tea. A cup of tea! \n\nI keep telling myself that a better husband—a guy who was actually paying attention, who actually took care of his wife the way he’s supposed to—he would have caught it so much sooner. He would have noticed something was really wrong and demanded they run tests, or... or just protected her somehow from all this pain she has to deal with now. I'm supposed to be the guy who fixes things. I can frame a whole house from the foundation up, but I couldn't even see that my own wife was getting sick right in front of my eyes. It’s just this constant, heavy guilt. I should have known, you know? I really should have done something to stop it, and I'll never forgive myself for missing all those signs.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge most of the time over these past two months?\n\nPatient: Anxious... well, I’ve been thinking about that word, actually. Because my sister, she gets anxious. She’s the type who, if the wind blows too hard, she thinks the whole roof is coming off. She’s always been like that, ever since we were kids. But for me... no, I wouldn't say I feel anxious or on edge. Not really. \n\nWhen I hear \"on edge,\" I think about being nervous, you know? Like you're just waiting for the other shoe to drop. Like when you're framing a roof and the weather's turning fast, and the wind is picking up, and you're just praying you can get the tarp down and secured before the skies completely open up and ruin the plywood. That’s being on edge. Your heart is pounding, you're looking over your shoulder, you're keyed up. But this? What I’ve been dealing with these last couple of months? It’s not like that at all. \n\nIt’s actually kind of the exact opposite, if that makes any sense. I’m not jumpy in the slightest. If the house phone rings, or if someone drops a whole stack of two-by-fours at the shop, I don’t even flinch. I just sort of... stare at it. I'm not sitting around biting my nails or worrying about what's going to happen tomorrow, because honestly, I barely have the energy to even care about tomorrow. \n\nAnd I know that pacing I told you about earlier, with the hand-wringing and wearing a hole in the floorboards... You can't really be on the edge of the cliff if you've already fallen all the way down to the bottom. So, no, I really wouldn't say anxious is the right word for it at all. It's just that heavy, awful guilt I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms improved or went away completely over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Improved? Gosh, I wish. No, not... not for a single minute, really. I keep waiting for that morning where I wake up and things feel a little lighter, you know? Like when a storm finally breaks and the barometric pressure drops. Because usually, if a guy gets into a funk, you at least have a good day mixed in there somewhere. A Friday where you knock off work early and the sun is shining and suddenly you feel like yourself again. But these last two months? It’s just been this one, long, continuous slog. \n\nThere hasn't been a single afternoon where I felt like the old me. I wake up at 5:15 every morning—I've always been an early riser, never really needed an alarm clock, just my body's natural rhythm after thirty years of construction—and before my feet even hit the bedroom floor, I'm already doing this mental check. I tell myself that maybe today is the day my legs won't feel like lead. Maybe today I won't just sit on the edge of the mattress staring at my boots for twenty minutes before I can even figure out how to put them on. But then I go to stand up, and... nope. It's right there waiting for me. Every single day. \n\nEven on the weekends, doc. You'd think, okay, Saturday rolls around, you don't have to go to the job site, you can just putter around the garage, maybe fix that squeaky hinge on the back door that's been bothering my wife... but I can't even manage that. The heaviness, the guilt I was talking about, the pacing... none of it takes a day off. I honestly can't point to a single hour, let alone a whole day, in the last eight or nine weeks where I felt like things were lifting or going away. It's just been a straight, flat line right at the absolute bottom, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of actually taking your own life, or made any plans to do so?\n\nPatient: Taking my own life... gosh. That's... well, I want to be completely honest with you here, doc. As far as making actual plans? Like, sitting down and figuring out a way to do it, or going out to the garage and looking for a way to end it all? No. I haven't done anything like that. I haven't made any plans. \n\nBut... the thoughts. That's a different story. I've definitely had thoughts. It's usually at night, or in that early morning time I was telling you about, when I'm just sitting there on the edge of the bed staring at my boots. I catch myself thinking that maybe... maybe it would just be easier if I did not wake up. Like, if I could just go to sleep and the good Lord just decided it was my time, and I didn't have to open my eyes and face another day of this... this crushing weight. It just feels like it would be such a relief to not have to carry it anymore, to not have to face that guilt. \n\nAnd then my mind, it just keeps spinning, and I start looking at my wife, and how much she's dealing with, and I actually catch myself thinking that my family might be better off without me. If the house phone rings, or if someone drops a whole stack of two-by-fours at the shop, I don’t even flinch. I just sort of... stare at it. I'm not sitting around biting my nails or worrying about what's going to happen tomorrow, because honestly, I barely have the energy to even care about tomorrow. \n\nAnd I know that pacing I told you about earlier, with the hand-wringing and wearing a hole in the floorboards... I know that probably sounds like I'm a nervous wreck, but my head isn't actually filled with anxious, panicky thoughts when I do it. It’s just this dumb, frustrating physical restlessness. But emotionally? Mentally? I'm not on edge. I feel more like... well, like I'm already at the bottom of the canyon, you know what I mean? You can't really be on the edge of the cliff if you've already fallen all the way down to the bottom. So, no, I really wouldn't say anxious is the right word for it at all. It's just that heavy, awful guilt I was telling you about.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your symptoms improved or went away completely over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Improved? Gosh, I wish. No, not... not for a single minute, really. I keep waiting for that morning where I wake up and things feel a little lighter, you know? Like when a storm finally breaks and the barometric pressure drops. Because usually, if a guy gets into a funk, you at least have a good day mixed in there somewhere. A Friday where you knock off work early and the sun is shining and suddenly you feel like yourself again. But these last two months? It’s just been this one, long, continuous slog. \n\nThere hasn't been a single afternoon where I felt like the old me. I wake up at 5:15 every morning—I've always been an early riser, never really needed an alarm clock, just my body's natural rhythm after thirty years of construction—and before my feet even hit the bedroom floor, I'm already doing this mental check. I tell myself that maybe today is the day my legs won't feel like lead. Maybe today I won't just sit on the edge of the mattress staring at my boots for twenty minutes before I can even figure out how to put them on. But then I go to stand up, and... nope. It's right there waiting for me. Every single day. \n\nEven on the weekends, doc. You'd think, okay, Saturday rolls around, you don't have to go to the job site, you can just putter around the garage, maybe fix that squeaky hinge on the back door that's been bothering my wife... but I can't even manage that. The heaviness, the guilt I was talking about, the pacing... none of it takes a day off. I honestly can't point to a single hour, let alone a whole day, in the last eight or nine weeks where I felt like things were lifting or going away. It's just been a straight, flat line right at the absolute bottom, day in and day out.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had thoughts of actually taking your own life, or made any plans to do so?\n\nPatient: Taking my own life... gosh. That's... well, I want to be completely honest with you here, doc. As far as making actual plans? Like, sitting down and figuring out a way to do it, or going out to the garage and looking for a way to end it all? No. I haven't done anything like that. I haven't made any plans. \n\nBut... the thoughts. That's a different story. I've definitely had thoughts. It's usually at night, or in that early morning time I was telling you about, when I'm just sitting there on the edge of the bed staring at my boots. I catch myself thinking that maybe... maybe it would just be easier if I did not wake up. Like, if I could just go to sleep and the good Lord just decided it was my time, and I didn't have to open my eyes and face another day of this... this crushing weight. It just feels like it would be such a relief to not have to carry it anymore, to not have to face that guilt. \n\nAnd then my mind, it just keeps spinning, and I start looking at my wife, and how much she's dealing with, and I actually catch myself thinking that my family might be better off without me. I mean, I'm supposed to be the provider, the one taking care of her, and instead I'm just this useless lump who can't even get his act together. I think to myself, maybe she wouldn't have to worry about me on top of her own health issues if I was just gone. Maybe the life insurance money would do more for her than I can right now. \n\nAnd I've got to tell you, doc, having thoughts like that... it scares me. It scares the living daylights out of me. I've always been a fighter, a guy who builds things up, you know? I frame houses to last a hundred years. I'm a protector. I don't tear things down. So to have my own brain telling me that my wife would be better off if I was dead... it's terrifying. It really rattles a man to his core to realize his mind is going to a place that dark. So, no plans, but... yeah, those thoughts are there, and they're really, really hard to shake once they get inside your head.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any noticeable changes in your physical activity level or movement over the past two months?\n\nPatient: Physical movement... yeah, well, I guess I kind of touched on that a little bit earlier when I mentioned the pacing, didn't I? But it's... it's actually gotten to the point where other people are pointing it out. The guys at the job, they've definitely noticed that something is really wrong with how I'm moving. It's like I've got two completely different speeds lately, and neither of them is any good for getting a house built. \n\nHalf the time, I'm moving like my boots are full of wet cement. I mean, I'll be standing there at the chop saw, and it literally takes me forever just to bend down and pick up a single board. Usually, I'm the guy who's two steps ahead of everyone else on the crew, right? I've got the cut list in my head, I'm moving fast, boom, boom, boom. But these past couple of months, it's like I'm dragging a hundred pounds of dead weight with every step I take. The younger guys will be waiting on me for a piece of trim or something, and I just can't get my body to cooperate at a normal speed. \n\nAnd then, if I'm not moving in slow motion like that, I get stuck in that other gear I was telling you about. I'll just be pacing around the shop, back and forth, rubbing my hands together over and over. Just completely stuck in this loop where I'm not actually doing anything useful. I'll spend an hour just walking circles around the sawhorses, wringing my hands, not cutting a single piece of wood, not driving a single nail. It's just embarrassing, honestly, having the crew see me like that. I've been doing this for thirty years, I should be leading the site, but instead, I'm either frozen in wet cement or pacing a trench in the concrete without getting a lick of real work done.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms been constant every day for the past two months, or have they come and gone?\n\nPatient: Like I was saying before, doc, it's been completely constant. Every single day. But to give you the exact timeline... this whole change in my mood, the behavior, the pacing, all of it... it's been going on for right about two months now. I can pinpoint it almost to the exact day, actually, because that’s right when my wife’s health suddenly declined. \n\nIt just... it came completely out of nowhere, really. One day we're going about our normal lives, and the next, everything is just turned upside down. And you have to understand, we are creatures of habit, her and me. For thirty years, we've had our routine. I get up at 5:15, she usually has the coffee going while I'm lacing up my boots, I head out to the job site, she does her thing around the house, I come home, we have dinner. It was like clockwork, you know? A good, solid, dependable routine. You build a life that way, just like you build a house, on a solid foundation. \n\nBut when her health took that sudden hit two months ago, all of that just... stopped. The whole routine completely changed overnight. Suddenly she can't do the things she used to do, and I'm trying to figure out how to manage taking care of her and keeping up at the job site, and... well, it was right then. That's exactly when all of this started hitting me. The bottom just fell out. My mood dropped off a cliff, and my behavior changed into whatever this is now, and it hasn't let up for a second since our routine broke. So yeah, two solid months of this, day in and day out, without a break.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me if there was ever a two-week period during these past two months where you felt a little better, or where some of these symptoms you've been describing weren't as bad?\n\nPatient: No, doc, no. Not even close. Like I said, it's been constant. Every single day for two months. There hasn't been a two-week period where I felt better, there hasn't even been a two-*hour* period where I felt better. \n\nI know some guys, they get down in the dumps, and maybe they have a good weekend, you know? They watch the game, have a beer, and they snap out of it for a little bit. Or they go fishing and clear their head. But not me. This thing, whatever it is, it's just wrapped around me like a wet tarp, and it does not let up. \n\nI keep waiting for that morning where I wake up and the coffee smells good again, or I look out the window and think, \"Hey, it's a good day to frame a roof.\" I keep hoping I'll get just a little break from the pacing, or a break from feeling like my boots are made of lead, or a break from those terrible thoughts about my wife being better off without me. But it just doesn't happen. \n\nEvery morning, 5:15, my eyes open, and boom, it's right there waiting for me. The exact same heavy, crushing feeling. The exact same guilt. The exact same slowness. It's like being stuck in a rut that's so deep you can't even see over the edges, and the tires are just spinning and spinning, day after day, week after week. So no, no two-week breaks. No breaks at all. It's just been one long, unbroken stretch of this miserable, heavy feeling ever since her health went south.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any period shorter than two weeks where you felt completely back to normal and without any of these symptoms since they started?\n\nPatient: No, doc, I haven't. Not for two weeks, not for two days, not even for an afternoon. It's just... it's like I was just saying, it's just this one long, continuous stretch of misery. \n\nYou know, you hear people talk about having a good day or a bad day, right? Like, \"Oh, Tuesday was rough, but Wednesday was better.\" But for me, the days don't even have names anymore. It's just one continuous blur of this heavy, awful feeling. I'll be sitting on the edge of the bed, staring at my work boots, and I'll think, \"Maybe today's the day. Maybe today I'll lace these up and feel like myself again. Maybe I'll go to the site and actually want to talk to the guys, or maybe I'll come home and be able to be there for my wife the way she needs me to be.\" \n\nBut it never happens. Not once in these past two months. I haven't had a single moment where I felt like the old me—the guy who could build a house from the ground up and still have energy to cook dinner. It's just... it's always there. Like a shadow that won't go away, no matter how much light you shine on it. I haven't felt completely back to normal for a single second since this whole thing started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
You know, doc... I know I've been harping on these last two months, because that's when my wife got sick, the routine broke, and the bottom completely fell out. That's when it got really, really unbearable. But... when you ask it exactly like that, about feeling low and disconnected from the things I usually enjoy? If I really sit back and look at it honestly... this depression has actually been dragging on for about six months now. 

I didn't really put two and two together until just this second, but it started way before her health took a hit. I remember back in the early spring, we were framing out a custom build, beautiful cedar siding, the kind of intricate finish work I usually live for. I've been a carpenter a long time, and I've always loved the smell of cut cedar, you know? It's one of the best parts of the job. But I'd get to the site and just... nothing. I felt totally disconnected from the wood, from the crew, from everything. The guys would be joking around at lunch, eating their sandwiches, and I'd just be sitting in my truck, staring at the steering wheel, feeling this incredible, heavy low. 

At first, I kept telling myself I was just tired, or maybe it was just ordinary stress from the supply chain issues we were having with the lumber yard. You try to rationalize it, right? You tell yourself, "Oh, it's just a bad week, everyone gets a bad week, you just need to put your head down and push through." But a bad week ends. You get some sleep, the weekend rolls around, and you bounce back. 

But this didn't. It just kept dragging on and on, month after month, just sucking the joy out of every single thing I used to care about. It's been about six months now, which is definitely long enough that it no longer feels like just a bad week or ordinary stress. It's something else entirely, something that just settled into my bones half a year ago and refused to leave, long before the wheels completely came off two months ago.

[
  {

Like, \"Oh, Tuesday was rough, but Wednesday was better.\" But for me, the days don't even have names anymore. It's just one continuous blur of this heavy, awful feeling. You get some sleep, the weekend rolls around, and you bounce back. \n\nBut this didn't. It just kept dragging on and on, month after month, just sucking the joy out of every single thing I used to care about. It's been about six months now, which is definitely long enough that it no longer feels like just a bad week or ordinary stress. It's something else entirely, something that just settled into my bones half a year ago and refused to leave, long before the wheels completely came off two months ago."
